#6786. 28 Thoughtful Gifts Under $25 That Don't Feel Tacky, Useless, or Weird

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

You'll want to get something for yourself.

Gift-giving during the holidays is a delicate dance: you want to find the right item that feels thoughtful and cool but also doesn’t kill your budget or feel like something they already own. Our team is passionate about helping people find the right items, from hosting gifts to products you can bring to your next white elephant to even what to put in your family’s stocking stuffers. The topic we’re tackling? Best gifts under $25 that don’t feel tacky or cheap.

If you dig hard enough, there are actually plenty of unique, thoughtful, and fun finds for under this price point. Whether it be a martini-shaped candle, colorful oven mitts, or a deck of tarot cards, the variety of gift ideas ahead will work for your groups’ gifting needs. These nifty finds are proof that you can buy something that feels special without spending a ton. We’ll raise a glass to that.

For the person who always orders a martini, gift them this candle that nods to a dirty martini! It has notes of greens, herbs, and juniper to make their home smell fresh. However, they may not even burn it, to keep the wax intact for maximum cuteness on the table.

The sentimentalist in your life will appreciate a framed photo, especially if they’re moving away, just moved into a new home, or have been talking about filling their space with meaningful items.

Bedding and towel brand Hommey recently launched tableware, and the pieces are just as colorful and fun as their sheets. We love these two-toned bowls, perfect for olives, nuts, and other small snacks for gatherings.

Store-bought lighters aren’t the prettiest, so give them a case to hold their lighter in. This option from Etsy can be personalized, and we’re guessing they’ll want to proudly leave it out on their coffee table right next to their candle.

Everyone talks about their coffee setup, but can we take a moment to talk about a proper matcha setup? Their morning ritual at home isn’t complete without a fun bowl and whisk stand set, and this Anthro pairing comes in many colorful options.

With winter heading our way, a heated blanket is a must. This soft flannel option comes in a classic plaid pattern that oozes holiday vibes and has 10 adjustable heat settings ranging from 86°F to 122°F.

If you catch them watching tarot readings on TikTok and YouTube, you know it’s time to get their own deck. This one is perfect for beginners because it comes with thought starters and features cat illustrations for a lighthearted touch.

Whether they just moved into their first apartment or recently invested in an espresso machine, they will need espresso cups. We love this set because it comes with six earthy shades, so if they have a few guests over, everyone has a cup.

Not only does a pie bird look adorable in the middle of a piping-hot apple pie, but it makes it taste better too. Pie birds (or whistles) vent steam during the baking process, preventing the filling from boiling over and the crust from getting soggy.

We had to test this mini water bottle ourselves, and we found it's incredibly handy to take on the go. It's just the right size to fit in a pocket, holding your morning coffee or a drink for the beach.

For the friend who loves a vinyl dig or a new pressing of their favorite artist, help them keep their collection in tip-top shape. This cleaning kit stores easily in a record cabinet, and keeps even the dustiest records sounding brand-new.

To fill your new taper candle holders (and support a small business in the process), snag a set of these hand-poured beeswax taper candles. They add instant drama, and they're paraffin-free. Plus, they're unscented, so they won't clash with the scentscape of your home.

Art therapy can be a great way to release stress and celebrate your inner child. This takes that joy on the road, with a brush that you only need water to use, and a design that disappears over time. No mess, and no requirement to get every stroke perfect.

Fluted designs are back in, and this adorable vase is perfect to display a few choice blooms. The U-shaped design also works as standalone decor, if you just want it to be a striking sculpture.

Though this giant farfalle looks like the real thing, it actually smells more like a traditional candle, with notes of jasmine, neroli, and orange blossom. It also comes in a rigatoni shape, if that's more the recipient's thing.

Of course, you can gift a loved one a candle and a set of matches from the drugstore, but that's hardly any fun. Instead, grab a much more stylish version of the traditional matchbox that they can leave out on display. Worry not; there's a striker on the bottom. The matches, however, are not included!

Anyone on your list who appreciates the kitschier things in life will adore this ceramic soap dish shaped like a claw-foot bathtub. Just like an actual tub, there is a tiny hole for drainage so that the suds don't sit (and get moldy) in the basin.

As far as we're concerned, no one can ever have too many oven mitts. After all, they get stained quickly, so having a fresh, clean pair on hand is always a good idea.

There's no shame in being afraid of the dark, so we say embrace the fear and snag this charming (and portable) touch lamp. It gets brighter and dimmer with just the tap of a finger. It almost mimics candlelight, but unlike a flickering flame, it is safe to use all night.

It may be hard to believe that something so artistic is from Amazon, the place we buy our sponges, gym socks, and YETI water bottles, but here we are. You can rest assured that, unlike some of the other goods you may buy on Amazon, this bowl is not made in bulk. In fact, it's from one of the thousands of small businesses that sell their wares on the platform. How's that for a lucky find?

#6782. Ezra Frech and Connor Wood Are the Final Boys

The last two men standing on DWTS talk beating expectations, blocking out the noise, and what happens to their bromance after the ballroom.

One of the best things to come out of Dancing With the Stars season 35? After Guillermo Rodriguez’s assisted knee walks and the chaotic Cat in the Hat dance routine following Tyler Cameron’s baby gender reveal, it’s got to be Ezra Frech and Connor Wood’s unexpected bromance.

Despite premiering over 21 years ago, the show is currently experiencing a major modern resurgence, and social media engagement is to thank. The show has tapped into younger audiences both with its casting choices and through daily behind-the-scenes clips, to the point where even A-listers like Jennifer Lawrence can recognize Connor drinking a smoothie on the street. (By the way, if you’re wondering whether J. Law has reached out to him since he paid her no mind, the answer is sadly no. “I beat that horse to death,” Connor says. “I’m sure she’s like, ‘Can this guy let it go?’”)

And it’s also thanks to social media that fans have gotten a front-row seat to Connor and Ezra’s off-camera bond. What began with a TikTok poking fun at the fact that Connor’s nickname is Fibula and Ezra was born without his left fibula (which was all Ezra’s idea, according to Connor) has blossomed into an iconic friendship. Now, the besties must face off as the two remaining male stars heading into the finale—but they’re not going to let a little competition come between them.

“Ezra is definitely more competitive,” Connor, who’s paired with Rylee Arnold, tells Cosmopolitan. “He’s got a fighting spirit. When we get to eliminations, I’m thinking about my post-show quesadilla. I mean, obviously I want to stay in the competition. But I’ve considered every week that I’ve made it a win. The only person I’m competitive with is myself because I want to get this right.”

“This is a competition show, but you’re kind of rooting for everybody else and want everyone else to do well,” Ezra, who’s partnered with Daniella Karagach, adds. “Plus, there’s so much that’s out of your control. I want Connor to succeed. He wants me to succeed. You know, it’s real love.”

The first time the two met was at the promo photo shoot ahead of the season premiere, where the connection was so instantaneous that Connor thought they must already know each other. As Connor explains, “We’re hugging, we’re chatting, and then I’m walking away like, ‘Oh my gosh, where did I meet him before?’ Turns out, it was our first time meeting. We just connected straight off the bat.”

“Sometimes you’ll meet somebody and just click and know like, ‘Oh, that’s somebody I’m gonna be friends with,’” says Ezra of that fateful first meeting. “I’m sure Connor and I would have definitely hit it off and become really close in any other context—but I think Dancing With the Stars accelerated that timeline because we’re both way out of our comfort zone. For us to be in this together, it’s bonded us in a very unique way.”

They may be a decade apart in age (Connor is 31 as of September, while Ezra was born a month before DWTS first premiered in 2005) and come from two entirely different worlds (Connor is a podcaster and comedian, while Ezra is a two-time Paralympian with two gold medals to prove it), but their friendship? From post-rehearsal dance parties to spray tan dates to trailer talks, it knows no bounds.

Cosmopolitan caught up with Ezra and Connor ahead of the DWTS season 35 finale to chat about beating the odds, blocking out the noise, and what happens after they leave the ballroom.

Connor: I thought there was more to the sentence. I thought I would hear, “Connor and Rylee, come down here and dance for us again.” I literally walked backstage after they called mine and Rylee’s name, and I took off my jacket and just sat on the ground. I was in shock.

Ezra: They do say names are called in no particular order, but when I was standing there with Harry [Shum Jr.], I was just like, “Wow, this is crazy.” No one will tell you that they’re standing up there feeling confident, so when they leave you up there longer, it definitely makes you sweat.

Connor: I’m an extrovert, but it’s very selective. I don’t do emotions. Being on the show, I’ve put out a more vulnerable me. I’ve been sharing things on national TV that I don’t even share with my friends, and it’s been wildly uncomfortable in the best way. You know, I’ve always found work for myself that’s come really easy to me—like, comedy has been natural for me. This is nothing like that. This is the hardest I’ve ever worked in my life, and my brain chemistry has been changed forever because of this.

Ezra: I didn’t come in with any dance experience, and quite frankly, I thought I had natural limitations on what I was capable of on the dance floor. What Dani has brought out of me is almost rewiring the limitations I placed on myself and changing my own expectations of what I can do on a prosthetic—which is crazy, because I’ve lived 21 years and felt like I had a pretty good understanding of what I was capable of on my own prosthetic leg. Then I come on Dancing With the Stars and meet Dani and that completely shifts.

Connor: [laughs] You think I’m watching myself? Oh, no. I don’t watch. And the choreo has been hard, but it’s gotten easier each week for me to learn dances. Like, learning the week 3 samba, I’m like, “Rylee, pack your bags. It’s been great getting to know you.” But now I know that I’ll get it.

Ezra: Yeah, I don’t really watch. I mean, I end up seeing clips, and earlier in the season, I would rewatch the dances. But everything’s happening so fast; it’s hard to, like, really sit and marinate on them when you gotta refocus for the next week. The cool thing is these dances will live forever. They’re out there in the world, and I will be able to watch and appreciate those moments and have pride in them for the rest of my life.

Connor: Michael Scott said it best: “Do I need to be liked? Absolutely not. I like to be liked. I enjoy being liked. I have to be liked.” I relate to that so much, because I’ll want to reach out to someone and be like, “Do you really hate me? Like, walk me through what I did during my jive that has made you just despise me?” I don’t think these people actually have that much hatred in their heart. I think they’re really invested fans, and it’s part of the game. Would I slide into someone’s DMs and say, “Watch your back”? No. But I know it’s what I signed up for.

Ezra: People online—as far as whether they think you’re great or mediocre or suck or whatever the opinions are on social media—shouldn’t influence your day-to-day. Thankfully, for Dani and I, there haven’t been too many negative comments, but, of course, with every 30 positive comments, you’re going to get a negative comment. It’s all about not letting that little negative comment overshadow the beautiful stuff that people are saying online as well.

Connor: This is a once-in-a-lifetime chance, but it’s also objectively fun. It’s a show about dancing. I’m not going to make a joke of it, but there’s humor involved. It’s so important to take a step back and laugh sometimes. This is fun. It should be fun.

Connor: You do it anxious. You know, there are people like Ezra with serious uphill battles, and he’s crushing it. I’m just here with anxiety and ADHD and small amounts of OCD and dancing on TV. It’s just something that I’ve always dealt with. I go into standup anxious, and honestly, I think it helps me operate. It pushes me to make sure I get in all of my rehearsal hours so that there’s nothing to be anxious about. As my dad told me, “If you weren’t anxious, you would be anxious.”

Ezra: I think the way we phrase things in our head controls the way we feel about them. Yes, it can be a burden to feel like you’re representing a community, but it’s a beautiful burden, and so I don’t see it as a negative thing. I get to play a small role in representing my community on such a special show like Dancing With the Stars—I think it’s actually, like, the pride and joy and honor of my life.

Ezra: No, it’s not in my DNA to chill. I mean, after 2028, I’ll probably take some downtime for sure—but then, you know, you’ve got the next Games in 2032, you’ve got world championships the next year, so there’s never really an end. I’ll probably compete on the track for another 15 years or so. And hopefully after the show ends, I’ll take a nice vacation or just rot for a little bit.

Connor: Rylee and Ezra are the same age, and you know, I do feel my age a lot of the time when I talk to them. Like, Rylee only knew who Nicole Kidman was because of the AMC pre-roll ads, and Ezra hadn’t seen High School Musical when we were doing the Wildcats dance. But both of them are so wise beyond their years. As soon as Ezra and I became friends, I started seeing old clips of him at, like, 9 years old on Ellen, and he was probably more eloquent and emotionally mature than I am now. People keep wanting him to be the Bachelor, but I think he needs to run for office. He’s just got this natural charisma.

Ezra: I didn’t listen to his podcast, but I’d seen clips of him online, and I knew of him. There’s a really funny video of a Team USA thing that I did two or three years ago where I’m lip-syncing to Connor’s audio. It’s like, “I have purse; I have shoe.” And now people are like, “Wow, the invisible string theory brought them together.” It’s funny; he’s the exact same online versus in person. He’s the same dude. He’s able to take things in, he’s able to feel them, and then he’s able to articulate them in a way that resonates with people. That’s what makes him so good as a comedian.

Connor: There’s this guy in a suit that’s jumping straight up in the air and it looks like an exclamation point. I’d say that’s Ezra, since he’s so elevated in the way he carries himself. And, of course, he’s got hops.

Ezra: I would describe Connor with the emoji that’s smiling and sweating, because he’s always smiling and he’s always kind of sweating, and it’s sort of perfect. Like, he’ll make jokes and then look around like, “Ooh, did that land?”

Connor: Val [Chmerkovskiy] and Jenna [Johnson] and I are gonna work on hip-hop dance at their studio. Val and I are also doing a standup show together at some point. I just adore Brandon [Armstrong] and Alan [Bersten], too. Us three together? Trouble. Britt [Stewart] and I connected on some zen woo-woo stuff, so we’re gonna hang out. And Jenna Dewan and her partner Steve [Kazee], we’re gonna hang out. Julianne [Hough] and I are gonna hang out in New York. I mean, I talk to everybody. I hope I see everybody again.

Ezra: Amber [Glenn] is someone who is a real close part of my life and will be for a long time. I feel like her and I are on the same team together in this with Pasha [Pashkov] and Dani. Guillermo is someone I’m going to be close to forever. Like me, him, and Connor—we’re in the group chat texting all the time. And I love Tyler Cameron, and Harry is the sweetest, and Jenna—like, it’s hard to pick and choose. I could see myself still talking to pretty much everybody on the cast.

Ezra: The internet is crazy. Right now, I’m definitely single but not necessarily looking for a relationship. Because, again, once the dance season ends, I’ll be committing myself entirely to track again. I’m enjoying being single, and it’ll probably stay that way for a while.

Connor: This has been like a study abroad trip. Like, I am fully immersed in a culture that is so far outside of my comfort zone and so far away from what I know. Learning something new at my age is so rare, let alone at this level on TV with a world-class athlete teaching me. And if it does happen, it probably happens to billionaires’ kids who are, like, paying Serena Williams to do private lessons for two years. I think I’m gonna wake up on Wednesday feeling like I walked out of a loud party and shut the door with my ears still ringing. Like, I’m alone. Party is over. It’s time to go home.

Ezra: I’ll miss getting to see everyone at the rehearsal studio. I’ll miss getting lunch with Dani after practice. I’ll miss getting the opportunity to commit myself to something like this so entirely. And I’m gonna miss the show days. You become so close to everybody, and you spend so much time around these people, and then all of a sudden, it’s going to end. But the good thing is that these people are still going to be in my life. I’m still going to hang out with Dani, and I’m still going to see Connor whenever I can, and we’ll get lunch with Guillermo. These are people I’ll be with forever.

Styled by Kaia Carioli. Sittings editor: Marissa Diaz. Grooming by Jaime Maloney for Bumble and Bumble.

#6781. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Technology directors touch nearly everything that happens in a school system. Here are three strategies these IT leaders can use to navigate their many duties without losing strategic focus, influence, or sustainability.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

SMART Technologies has introduced SoundFlow, a plug-and-play broadcasting system that allows classroom audio content to flow directly from a SMART Board display to students' compatible hearing aids, earbuds, headphones, personal listening devices, or speakers.

→ Baca artikel asli

#6780. Before & After: Two Best Friends Turned a Stripped-Down Tudor Into a Colorful, Antique-Filled Dream Home

Designer Anne Hulcher Tollett understood her client’s signature aesthetic better than anyone.

Anne Hulcher Tollett can’t remember a life without her best friend, Van. They met in a playpen as toddlers and quickly became an integral part of each other's lives, with many childhood memories taking place in Van’s house growing up. Tollett has been with her best friend through every milestone, including as her maid of honor at her wedding to her husband, Sean, and when the couple put an offer on a suburbanized Tudor home in Richmond, Virginia.

The five-bedroom house had good bones and an intuitive flow, but it needed help getting some of its charm back. When Van and Sean bought it for their family of four (plus their dog, Jelly Bean, and a small flock of chickens), it was whitewashed and lacked architectural character. In short, it wasn’t up to par with Van’s eclectic style, according to Tollett, but as the principal designer of Hanover Avenue, she reassured her bestie that she could easily take it from suburban chic to warm, layered, and personality-filled without having to completely rework the interior.

“Van’s aesthetic is diametrically opposed to ‘simple’ or ‘builder-grade,’ and although this house had been meticulously renovated and maintained by the previous owners, some of the charm she loves had been removed,” says Tollett. The designer and her team focused on lighter renovations and cosmetic upgrades, walking her best friend through every decision, from wallpaper choices to reworking the staircase to highlight the amazing treasures Van has collected on her travels over the years.

Throughout the renovation, Tollett and Van leaned into their sisterly shorthand, resulting in a project with very few disruptions. “Our biggest challenge was creating unexpected quirky moments without taking down walls or undoing all of the thoughtful and well-built upgrades that came with the house,” Tollett explains. “We found places on tables, walls, and bookshelves to highlight everything Van and her family have collected over the years. It makes the house feel like theirs and not the product of some design firm.”

Walls were covered with grasscloth, patterns were layered on throw pillows and vintage rugs, and anything that wasn’t antique or pre-owned was chosen to add a touch of patina to the space, allowing Van’s collections to truly sing.

Once Tollett was finished with the renovation, the house told the story of their friendship and a life well lived. Because the designer knows Van like the back of her hand, she was able to create a retreat that feels like a true reflection of who she is and how her family lives without editing out any of Van’s eccentricities. “We understood her design sensibility and took something well-built but fairly plain and made it fun, pretty, and interesting at every turn, which is exactly how anyone would describe Van!” Tollett adds. Besides, who better to design your house than the person who knows you better than yourself?

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne Hulcher Tollett of Hanover AvenueLocation: Richmond, VirginiaThe Space: A 4,600-square-foot Tudor home with five bedrooms, four full bathrooms, and two half-baths

The barrel back and turquoise chairs were finds that Tollett sourced for Van’s previous home, proving that timeless profiles and accessible sizes can work in many different spaces throughout their lifetimes.

“A room like this with a vaulted ceiling and white walls creates a wonderful backdrop for bold pops of color and patterns because it can visually hold its own against furnishings with personality,” she says. “Van had the beautiful sideboard, and we loved pairing it with the tattooed table.”

Possibly one of the most remarkable makeovers in the entire house, Tollett coated the entryway in deep blue paint, topping it off with darling wallpaper on the ceiling for added dimension. “This is a great example of how a can of paint, a roll of wallpaper, and some strategically placed treasures can transform a room,” she says.

Large-scale grasscloth wallpaper instantly gave the once-all-white box character and rhythm, setting a serene backdrop for the rest of the unique finds in this dining room. The chandelier hanging over the table had long been on Tollett’s list to use in a project, and Van’s home was the perfect spot.

“The dining room is right near the front door, so it’s hung to look like it's being blown by the entrance breeze—a true classic with a twist!” the designer says.

Above the antique bar (whose natural patina plays so well with the wallpaper), are two framed photos of puppets, which Van found during one of her globe-trotting trips. She carried them around in her backpack, and Tollett loved the story behind them so much that she had them framed. On the opposite wall, behind the piano, is a portrait of Van’s grandmother in a vibrant green dress.

The kitchen was well-maintained when Tollett got her hands on it, but it didn’t feel all that fun, and it needed more than simple cosmetic refreshes like other areas of the home.

“We removed the big-box island lighting and reworked the storage, hood, and casework on the range wall,” Tollett explains. “We did the same on the wall opposite the range,” she adds, which was in fantastic shape, so they kept it.

To give the cook space more character, the designer installed a custom Delftware tile backsplash. The island was also in good shape, so Tollett upgraded the hardware, faucet, face frames, and lighting above it, opting for three Pinch pendant lights which bring an interesting, natural texture to the space.

“Open concept kitchens are tricky because they are seen from every angle, so we knew we needed to improve the storage to hide clutter,” she adds. Opposite the range wall, Tollett removed the open shelves, creating closed casework instead and topping everything off with pretty hardware and colors that play well with the adjacent dining and living rooms.

Tollett knew she was going to wrap this powder room in a beautiful butterfly-and-bird wallpaper, so she custom-matched a paint to fully drench the room in a whimsical emerald green hue. They even had their wonderful wallpaper installers cut out a few extra birds and butterflies to extend the design onto the ceiling, creating a more immersive experience. “We wanted the powder room to pack an equal punch to the bold, blue entryway it connects to,” she explains.

One of the mantras Tollett and her team at Hanover Avenue follow is “wonderfully weird,” which the hand sculpture extending through the wall to hold the soap perfectly captures. “The space feels not only more fun but also much more personalized to our clients!”

During the renovation, Van’s mother passed away, so Tollett transformed this almost-forgotten area into an homage to the matriarch. “We reused her beautiful plate rack and hung all her flawless collections of plates on the walls,” the designer says, officially turning this room, which overlooks the vegetable gardens, into the “plate room.”

They papered the walls in warm neutral grasscloth and hung relaxed Roman shades to softly filter the natural light, furnishing the room with many of the fabulous pieces Van already owned (and loved). “This is a wonderful example of ‘reimagining what you have,’ and finding ways to remember the ones we love in our own rooms,” Tollett adds. “Van’s mom was the coolest!”

“Decades of sleepovers in Van’s childhood bedroom allowed me to execute exactly what she was hoping to achieve,” Tollett explains. Though there are many layers of pattern in this sitting area, the designer managed to prevent it from feeling busy by keeping the same serene color palette for most of the textiles, minus a few pops of red for a warm balance.

“This room is a grown-up translation of Van’s entire design sensibility that began when she was young and was nurtured in her adulthood during her travels,” Tollett adds.

On the other side of the room is a calming wash of warm white paint and natural textures, giving Van a visual place of rest ideal for sleeping. Tollett calls it a “well-collected, well-traveled, layered space that seems to have evolved all on its own—a sanctuary for Van and Sean,” which makes the designer especially happy since she was the maid of honor at the couple’s wedding.

The primary bath really only needed some cosmetic upgrades, but they did a lot of heavy lifting. Airy Roman shades soften the space while Van’s mother’s bench and one of her existing rugs give it personality. By opting for two warmer paint colors and swapping out key fixtures, such as the lights, hardware, and faucets, Tollett created a serene, sophisticated escape that looks completely new, all with the same bones.

Tollett tapped into her childhood memories with her best friend when designing this room. “During slumber parties, Van and I fell asleep across from each other in twin beds, so it felt fitting to create another bedroom that fosters the same kind of childhood chatter,” she says, adding that stepping inside is like stepping into a time machine going back to 1983.

Along with the beds, much of the look was directly inspired by Van’s childhood bedroom, the round green pillow on the bed having been plucked directly from it. The pre-existing Tudor windows were perfect for the vintage vibe.

Tucked away at the far end of the home is a spot connected to the sunroom lounge. Dubbed the “dayroom”—an informal spot dedicated to relaxation and reading—Tollett wanted it to feel cozy and as collected as the rest of the home but still a bit private. They washed the walls in a soft gray-green hue and did the same to the custom day bed, finishing off the room with antique bookcases in a complementary gray tone. It’s easy for small rooms like these to feel overcrowded, but the pale color palette takes away some visual weight.

As founder and principal designer of Hanover Avenue, Anne Hulcher Tollett is intimately involved in every project the Richmond-based design firm takes on, from high-end residential to boutique commercial. Tollett finds a thrill in uncovering her clients’ design styles and endless joy in creating their dream homes that reflect who they are.

#6778. Where Does Sunrise on the Reaping Rank Among the 6 Hunger Games Movies?

Warning: Spoilers for all six Hunger Games movies.

With the release of Sunrise on the Reaping, Suzanne Collins continues to expand on The Hunger Games movie franchise. The latest installment tells the story of Haymitch Abernathy’s Hunger Games—which doubles as the Second Quarter Quell, where twice the number of tributes are reaped—and details his journey from a humble member of District 12 to one of the most important forces in the Second Rebellion by the time Katniss Everdeen’s story is told. But how does Sunrise on the Reaping stack up against the other five films? Read on for ELLE’s ranking below.

Catching Fire ends with a major cliffhanger. When Katniss shoots the arrow that breaks the 75th Hunger Games arena, it kick-starts the true beginning of the revolution. It also provides one of the most heartbreaking plots in the series—when Peeta gets left behind during Katniss’s rescue from the arena, she’s forced to continue her rebel duties without having Peeta by her side.

The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes tells the story of Lucy Gray Baird, the first District 12 winner of the Hunger Games, whose Games have been erased from history. The film also provides much-needed insight into President Snow’s background, setting the stage for his years as a ruthless authoritarian. To restore his family’s reputation, Snow (Tom Blyth) mentors Lucy Gray (Rachel Zegler) in the 10th Hunger Games, but ends up forming an attachment to her that turns romantic. After Lucy wins the Hunger Games, she runs away with Snow so they can have a future without Capitol rule—but when she catches him in a lie that hints at his dangerous nature, she flees. The betrayal haunts Snow for the rest of his life and inspires him to make the Hunger Games even more vicious.

Before Katniss, there was Haymitch Abernathy’s Hunger Games. The second of the prequel movies, Sunrise on the Reaping shows Haymitch (Joseph Zada) before he became the jaded, embittered drunk that we meet in the first Hunger Games film. Haymitch isn’t entirely disillusioned by the government’s treatment of the district, but being forced to enter the games under such intense circumstances radicalizes him so much he’s inspired to take action. His hopes to spearhead the rebellion don’t pan out the way he had hoped, but that failure paves the way for a future where another rebel could be successful.

The dramatic conclusion to the entire franchise. In Mockingjay: Part 2, the conflict between President Snow (Donald Sutherland), the Capitol, and the outlying districts comes to a head, with the rebels working towards ending his authoritarian reign. Katniss, backed by her own personal army, successfully infiltrates the Capitol and finds it turned into a massive Hunger Games arena before she can successfully capture and kill Snow. The final film isn’t afraid to deliver surprising punches, with Prim dying while helping children in the Capitol and Katniss killing Alma Coin when Snow was meant to be her original target.

The movie that starts it all. Viewers are introduced to the world of Panem and its poorest district, District 12, on the day of the reaping ceremony, in which one girl and one boy are selected to participate (and likely die) in the Hunger Games. Katniss’s sister, Prim, worries about being selected to fight in the Games in her first year of eligibility—when Prim is selected, Katniss bravely volunteers in her sister’s place and rekindles the fire of the rebellion by nearly eating poisonous berries with Peeta while under the impression only one of them could win.

Devoted fans and critics agree—Catching Fire is easily the best film of the Hunger Games franchise. After Katniss survives the 74th Hunger Games and returns home alongside Peeta, the stakes are higher and the challenges are more complex than they were during her first trip to the arena. After being reaped for the Third Quarter Quell, Katniss finds herself fighting among skilled victors while unknowingly being at the center of a plot to overthrow the government. What follows is the true beginning of the rebellion and the battle against the Capitol and President Snow.

#6776. How a Connected Home Supports a Good Night’s Rest

How a wellness content creator uses Hubspace from The Home Depot to curate an environment for better sleep.

As a certified holistic health coach and wellness content creator, Carolina Salazar knows the importance of a peaceful wind-down routine before bed. She trusts Hubspace from The Home Depot that powers many of the smart devices in her home, to help ease her into a good night’s sleep.

Hubspace, The Home Depot’s exclusive smart home platform, powers more than 250 products across select private brands—like Hampton Bay, Husky, and Vissani—as well as national brand partners. It effortlessly connects everyday essentials, including smart security, indoor and outdoor lighting, appliances, switches, watering controls, and more.

Some of the Hubspace app’s most practical features help users personalize lighting and improve home security. Hubspace Connect, for example, lets you control compatible lighting from a single switch, while Vacation Mode is a security feature that turns lights and smart plugs on and off while you’re away. One of Salazar’s favorite features is Circadian Rhythm, which automatically transitions lights from cooler, whiter tones to warmer ones as the day goes on.

“I can set my lights to follow my circadian rhythm, so they shift automatically throughout the day,” says Salazar. “And when they start to turn warm in the evening, that’s my cue to slow it down.” That can mean cozying up with a book, brewing a cup of hot tea, or, in her case, lighting a candle at her bedside and journaling. When Salazar is finished putting her thoughts to paper, she uses the Hubspace app to adjust the color, temperature, and brightness of her lights, dimming them as she gets closer to sleep. This can even be programmed automatically in order to reduce screen time before bed.

“Colder rooms bring better sleep, so I also have Hubspace turn on my AC at night,” she says.

The Hubspace app is free to download and easily syncs with your home’s smart devices. You can enable voice control for screen-free commands—also a plus if you’re trying to reduce your pre-sleep screen time. And the app runs on both WiFi and Bluetooth—so even if the WiFi is down, your nighttime routine can still run seamlessly.

#6775. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6774. Kate Middleton Is Releasing Heal, a Documentary About Her Life After Cancer: Here’s What We Know

Kate Middleton is sharing a deeply personal look at life after cancer. On Saturday, November 21, the Princess of Wales released the first trailer for Heal, a new documentary that will explore the emotional realities of a cancer diagnosis, recovery, and finding hope in the face of uncertainty.

“Cancer does change you,” Kate said. “It changes your body. It changes you physically. But tHere’s a much, much deeper part to what a cancer diagnosis shifts in you.”

Kate first publicly shared her cancer diagnosis in March 2024, revealing that doctors had discovered cancer following major abdominal surgery two months earlier. At the time, she said she was undergoing preventative chemotherapy and that she and Prince William had taken time to explain the news to their three children before making it public. In September 2024, she announced that she had completed chemotherapy, while emphasizing that her recovery was ongoing. “My path to healing and full recovery is long and I must continue to take each day as it comes,” she wrote. In January 2025, she confirmed that her cancer was in remission, but noted that adjusting to a “new normal” takes time. Later that July, during a hospital visit, she spoke candidly about the challenges of life after treatment, explaining that the period following chemotherapy can be “really, really difficult” as patients adjust to life outside the clinical care system.

The documentary follows the princess as she takes on the National Three Peaks Challenge, climbing the highest mountains in Scotland, England, and Wales within 24 hours. Along the way, she reflects on her own experience and connects with others navigating serious illness. The trailer also offers glimpses of Kate embracing her children and husband, Prince William, as well as an emotional exchange with her younger brother, James Middleton.

“Isn’t it when you leave the Royal Marsden, you ring the bell?” James asked Kate, referring to the tradition of ringing a bell to mark the end of cancer treatment.

“Yeah, but I didn’t ring the bell,” she responded.

“So maybe this is your ringing of the bell,” he affirmed.

Through Heal, Kate hopes to help others navigate diagnosis, recovery, and life beyond cancer. According to the documentary’s official announcement, the princess also hopes to help transform access to holistic cancer care that supports well-being, resilience, and quality of life during and after a diagnosis. All funds received through the documentary’s distribution will go to The Royal Marsden Cancer Charity, which supports people living with and beyond cancer.

“Healing is a journey,” she says in the trailer. “How to reconnect with the things you once knew and how to find a sense of hope and joy alongside uncertainty.”

Here’s everything to know about Heal, including its release date and where to watch.

Heal explores the physical and emotional impact of serious illness, focusing on the ways people find comfort, strength, and hope during and after a cancer diagnosis. The film also highlights the importance of connection and holistic care, with Kate sharing her own reflections alongside conversations with others navigating illness.

The documentary was produced by Will Warr’s Detail Films and directed by Benji Jackson. Its original score was composed by Erland Cooper.

All funds received through the documentary's distribution will go to The Royal Marsden Cancer Charity. Kate and Prince William became joint patrons of the hospital in January 2025, following her treatment there.

Heal will premiere in the United States on Monday, November 30, at 8 P.M. ET on NBC. It will also air on Bravo at 11:30 P.M. ET, with streaming available on Peacock beginning Tuesday, December 1.

In the United Kingdom, the documentary will be available on BBC iPlayer beginning at noon on Sunday, November 29, before airing on BBC One that evening. It will premiere in Australia on Channel 9 and 9Now on Tuesday, December 1, and in Canada on CTV and Noovo on Monday, December 7, with streaming on Crave beginning December 8.

The first trailer for Heal was released on the Princess of Wales’s official Instagram account. Watch it below.

#6771. Take Note: This Is How Cool People Are Decorating for Thanksgiving This Year

These tastemakers are bringing their personal style to the holiday.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pumpkin centerpieces, rustic garlands, dried-fruit wreaths, and cornucopias are classic Thanksgiving decorations. They’re timeless and beloved, but they can also feel predictable. Traditional decor has its place, of course, but Thanksgiving tablescapes—and dinner parties in general—are evolving, much like interiors, to feel more personal, lived-in, and layered. So we asked nine tastemakers and experts how they’re decorating for this family-centric holiday.

From traditional touches to wholly unexpected looks, read on to see how they’re making their homes feel festive this autumn, and maybe you’ll be inspired to try something new this year.

Ever since designer Mikel Welch moved to sunny California, his family has never hesitated to go to his for Thanksgiving to escape the chilly Michigan weather. It’s become a tradition to gather at his house, and similarly to how he grew up, Welch likes to keep his decorations for the holiday understated and warm.

“I love getting the exterior of my house into full fall mode with mums, pumpkins, and seasonal greenery,” Welch tells House Beautiful. “The beauty of fall decorating is that it can carry you from October straight through Thanksgiving without having to change much.”

The designer’s house is filled with dark, moody colors and earthy textures year-round, so he focuses mainly on bringing natural elements inside to do the work for him. “I also have a little tradition with my dad,” he adds. “For the past couple of years, we've made trips to the nursery together to pick out flowers, greenery, and sometimes bales of hay for the front of the house. It's become something I really enjoy doing with him.”

“Thanksgiving has always been a big family affair,” real estate expert and maximalism extraordinaire Harvin Greene says. She’s happy to keep that tradition going as an adult, just in her own way. In the past, Greene’s aunt and uncle would have everyone at their house for the big meal, but last year, they passed the hosting baton to Greene for the first time. “I may have gotten a little carried away...in the best possible way.”

Getting to decorate two tables—one for the “adults,” another for the “kids,” even though they’re technically all grown-ups, too—Greene used this opportunity to design two completely different tablescapes. “I grew up surrounded by women who understood that a beautifully set table and a genuinely good time should go hand in hand,” she adds.

The one in the formal dining space (i.e., the “adults’” table) was theatrical and dramatic, and nearly everything on it had some sort of familial connection, from her parents’ engraved goblets to her husband’s grandmother’s sterling flatware. The kids’ table, on the other hand, was whimsical and vibrant, complete with a yellow patterned tablecloth, colorful glassware, and playful animal candlesticks. “I don't feel the need to follow the traditional Thanksgiving color palette,” Greene says. “Who decided Thanksgiving had to be orange and brown? Yellow, pink, turquoise, and green can be just as festive.”

The holiday isn’t over until they bring out the Bingo cards, a spirited Thanksgiving tradition that Greene’s Aunt Jean started—which involves cash prizes. “It's one of those family traditions that makes the holiday feel like ours, no matter whose house we're celebrating in.”

Talia Hubble is no stranger to a good dinner party—much of her content actually centers around her love of hosting. But Thanksgiving is a bit extra special for the Rhode Islander, especially since it’s being held at her cottage this year. “Growing up, it was just me and my mom, and she was too busy to decorate,” Hubble says. “I think that’s part of why I’ve fallen in love with seasonal decorating as an adult, I get to bring all my cozy childhood decorating dreams to life!”

As soon as the calendar switches from October to November, Hubble starts decorating for fall, opting for an eclectic mix of subtle Christmas decor (think bows, garlands, and wreaths), an excessive amount of taper candles, and a traditional Thanksgiving palette of oranges, browns, greens, and reds. Every year looks a little bit different, but there are pieces in her collection that are simply too cute not to bring out every holiday season.

“Half the fun of pulling everything out for Thanksgiving is remembering where it came from,” she says. “I think the best decor is a culmination of pieces you’ve been collecting for years, and the memories behind those pieces are what make the table feel special and uniquely yours.”

By the time she’s done decorating, her home feels like a warm, welcoming escape, somewhere her guests can exhale. “My philosophy is that you can’t go wrong when you aim for cozy, colorful, and embrace a little organized mess,” Hubble adds. “My favorite Thanksgiving tables are the ones that look even better halfway through dinner, when the napkins are rumpled and everyone’s reaching for seconds.”

Chef and food stylist Romilly Newman can’t always count on being in the same place as her family for Thanksgiving since everyone’s scattered about, but she always makes a point to throw an autumnal dinner with friends around the holiday time, complete with beautiful fall decor.

Her mom never decorated their home while she was growing up with specific Thanksgiving decorations—instead, she would decorate with deep orange and red flowers to capture the fall vibe. Newman’s style as an adult is similar. “I am fall-obsessed, so as soon as the temperature drops from summer, I'm setting up branches, candles, and darker accents,” she says. “I love the atmosphere inside and outside around Thanksgiving time, and I try my best to bottle that up in my home.”

Natural thicker linen, pops of silver, dark florals, and moody lighting envelop her space as the chef relies mostly on candlelight and organic elements—like figs, sage, chestnuts, and mandarins—as the decorative accents.

“Thanksgiving is great because it brings family together and offers time to enjoy food, friends, and being at home,” Newman adds. “Whatever your style is, try to create an environment that allows for great conversation and a festive evening. You want everyone to be comfortable and feel the holiday spirit.”

When writer Olivia Noceda was a child, she would stay up late the night before Thanksgiving and design custom menus for everyone on Microsoft Paint, which she would then print out, frame, and place on each table setting in preparation for the big meal. The holiday was a big deal for Noceda’s mother, who would always put on the most beautiful Thanksgiving gatherings. “I think that's where my love for hosting and setting a beautiful table really started,” she explains.

However, for the last six years, Noceda has been having Thanksgiving with her husband’s side of the family, so when she does host around the holiday, it’s typically for Friendsgiving. “I don't necessarily pick a theme, but I do like everything to feel cohesive,” she explains of her decorating process. “I usually start incorporating little autumnal touches sometime in October and let them carry me through Thanksgiving.”

The writer and recipe developer lets the season guide her, building her tablescape and decor around a beautiful branch from the farmers’ market, a bowl of pears or persimmons, a collection of little pumpkins and squash, or a few taper candles on the dining room table. Ingredients play a big part in her decor—she can even take a fresh loaf of bread and make it feel special on her table.

For the rest of her decor, she likes to incorporate vintage serving pieces, everyday elements, and elevated details like antique silver and linen napkins with things she already owns to lean into that lived-in, warm feeling. The last thing she wants is her home to feel like a department store display. “Nothing needs to match perfectly, and I actually prefer when it doesn't,” Noceda adds.

What entrepreneur and Real Housewives of New York star Erin Lichy remembers best about Thanksgivings growing up is the smell. “We always spent Thanksgiving out east, and the house was full of pumpkins, local hay, and lanterns,” she explains. The scent of that fresh produce, mixed with her grandmother’s homemade turkey t’bit, has shaped the way she decorates for and hosts Thanksgiving. “That coveted and highly personal (and incredibly delicious) recipe is in my cookbook, She’s a Host, and it’s part of every Thanksgiving I host.”

Decorating starts in the kitchen for her family. Lichy wants the home to smell like fall, so she focuses on that sense, making seasonal recipes with squash and pumpkin and incorporating autumnal flowers in her interiors. The melding of those scents gives her townhouse a warm feeling, especially when mixed with layered fall colors and the crackling of the fireplace.

Ever since Lichy and her family have moved into their townhouse, she’s been obsessed with decorating her stoop too. “The stoop is the first thing everyone sees, so this year I decided to bring in Simon Wallace Design to make it really stand out!” Lichy adds. “They completely transformed it with layers of seasonal color and texture, and it feels so warm and welcoming.” She specifically loves using the faux garlands and pinecones, as well as lanterns, from Balsam Hill and Frontgate because they’re so beautifully made that they look real, and she’s able to reuse them year after year.

No matter what she does to decorate though, she just wants her home to feel warm and lived-in, and this is the season and holiday to really lean into that.

Decorating for the holidays is Gabby Amato’s version of a self-care day. “It's truly one of my favorite days of the year,” she says. “I carve out a weekend afternoon when my husband and son are out of the house, light my favorite fall candles, turn on some music, and have the house entirely to myself.”

Amato, the founder of Table Two Studio, a paper plate brand which specializes in beautiful, artistic designs that you definitely won’t want to throw away, credits her love of Thanksgiving and the fall season in general to her mother. While growing up in the ‘90s, she saw her mom put so much care and effort into the holiday decorations, always making their home feel warm, nostalgic, and full of spirit. “For Thanksgiving, she decorated our mantel with gourds, greenery, and ceramic pilgrim statues that were a gift from my grandmother,” Amato says. “We had oversized cornstalks and pumpkins at the front door. Nothing ever felt overly styled or precious.”

It’s this feeling that has stuck with her throughout her life, and it’s what she hopes she captures in her own home every year. She begins decorating for the fall early, right after Labor Day, pulling out old pieces and unboxing anything new and arranging it all in fresh ways so nothing feels stale. “I love beautiful decor, of course, but I'm much more interested in creating an atmosphere than achieving a particular aesthetic,” she adds. “I want my home to feel warm, lived-in, a little nostalgic, and festive.”

Donna Lennard’s family was never one for holiday dressing—most of the attention at Thanksgiving fell on the delicious food shared with family. As a restauranteur and cofounder of beloved NYC establishment il Buco, it makes sense why the meal remains to be the most important thing to Lennard even today.

“Clearly, the table is always festive, whether it’s the tablecloth, the napkins, or linens that reflect the season in burnt orange and the like,” she explains. “I love to decorate a table with assorted colorful gourds and seasonal florals, beautiful candlelight (our beeswax candles already have an orange glow), orange ranunculus, and the occasional dried floral.” Colorful Pienza glassware dots the table and her plethora of beautiful tableware gives it more character, but she keeps everything else relatively simple to ensure the food remains the center of attention.

Though Lennard doesn’t often host her family and friends for the Thanksgiving meal, she’s already looking forward to next year when everyone will finally gather at her beach house.

Being from Australia, Kendall Knox—the cofounder of Olive Ateliers along with her husband—didn’t have a personal reference of what the holiday “should” be or look like. This gives her the freedom of deciding what it is for her family. “What it has become is an intimate table: my husband Ben, our son Rhodes, and Ben's mum,” she says. “No rotation, no kids' table, no forty-pound bird. Just the four of us and a dinner that runs long because nobody is in a hurry to leave it.”

Since Knox isn’t beholden to the classic Thanksgiving decor, she tends to keep her home and her table rather simple, “shopping the house,” as she puts it to decorate instead of buying something new. She keeps a rather neutral palette of rich browns, creamy whites, terracotta, and silver, then uses antique linens, mismatched silverware, and candlesticks that have collected wax to capture the true lived-in feel.

Her focus tends to be on the table and the meal, letting the food add more color. If a tablecloth has a wine ring on it from the year before, all the better—it complements the stacked plates at the end of the meal.

“The objects that come out every year, that get a little more marked each time, are the ones that end up meaning the most,” Knox adds. “Thanksgiving is just a very good excuse to use them.”

#6770. 7 Holiday Decor Trends Designers Say We're About to See Everywhere

The pros are leaning into handmade holiday style.

Pictured above: A holiday-ready space by designer Marie Flanigan.

Christmas is just around the corner, and as the holiday spirit makes its way into our homes, it’s a good time to give your seasonal decor a refresh. This year, on-trend doesn’t have to mean brand-new—designers love decorating with heirloom pieces collected over time.

However, if you’re in the mood to try something a little different, here are seven trends on our radar for a chic Christmas.

This winter, our decor is taking a note from European culture—again. Swedish folk art is emerging as a hot winter trend in both holiday and home decor. Defined by bold red hues, tulip motifs, and S-scrolls, the style blends cozy-core with the heritage style craze.

“European folk influences are so big right now,” says designer Johanna Lyle of Jo Lyle & Co. “Think Swedish Dala horses, quilt motifs, and decorative stitching. I think we will see those continue into holiday decor.”

When it comes to her tree decor, interior designer Francesca Grace waves a hand at the carefully contrived, and she’s not alone. “My ornaments are collected from my travels, so the tree is really a collection of memories. I love unpacking them and remembering where each one came from. They don’t all match, and I wouldn’t want them to,” says Grace. “I let the ornaments lead. That mix feels much more personal to me, and it gives the tree something new to say as the collection grows.” This year’s trends hinge on antiqued, inherited style, with one-off pieces that channel bold character and hardly ever come with a matching set.

Interior designer Marie Flanigan likes to add a fresh twist to her seasonal decor with dried oranges and pops of cranberry red.

“We've seen such a beautiful resurgence of dried citrus in holiday decorating over the past few years,” Flanigan explains. “And I think cranberries are ready for their moment to shine. Their rich red hue feels especially festive, and I love the idea of incorporating them into fresh greenery, draping them across a mantel, or pairing them with dried oranges for a more layered look.”

If there’s one classic Christmas element Grace hopes makes a comeback, it's multicolored lights. Inherently nostalgic, these bold-hued bulbs were a ’90s staple.

“I’m ready for a little less restraint on the tree. Those reds, blues, greens, and yellows immediately bring back the feeling of Christmas as a child. They also pull together a collection of ornaments that might otherwise seem completely unrelated. White lights can be beautiful, but I want my Christmas tree to have some mischief,” says Grace.

The classic red-and-green combination may take the title for the most iconic holiday palette, but this year, color trends are going complementary. While base colors stay warm and slightly rusted, patterned pieces are taking on cooler tones. This year, we’re also trading traditional plaid for Scottish tartan.

“Warm crimsons, rich browns, tactile fabrics, and blue-green plaids make a beautiful holiday palette,” says Elizabeth Bennett, cofounder of the interior design firm Kobel + Co.

In a total rejection of mass-manufactured everything, handmade felt pieces are taking holiday homes by storm. The style has even coined a new holiday aesthetic: #Kitschmas, and, according to Lyle, it’s here to stay. “As AI continues to homogenize design, things made by humans will feel more special and important. Handmade and crafted items create stories around decor that feel meaningful and can be treasured year after year,” she says.

Moody browns are steadily emerging as the new neutral as we inch closer to the new year, and lucky for our winter design schemes, the hue really is as versatile as it seems. Paired with powdery blues, bold crimsons, and even minty greens, chocolate brown grounds nearly any palette and matches every metallic hue.

“I’m particularly drawn to chocolate brown as an unexpected foundation for holiday color palettes. It's incredibly rich and versatile. I love the idea of moving beyond traditional red and green and allowing the existing colors of a room to inform the holiday decor. You can create something incredibly festive through tonal layering, subtle metallic accents, and variations in material,” Flanigan explains.

#6768. Meghan Markle and Prince Harry Have a Different Kind of Christmas Planned This Year

Meghan and Harry are preparing to spend Christmas in the U.K. with their children, Prince Archie and Princess Lilibet, and Meghan’s mother, Doria Ragland. The holiday will mark the first time Meghan and Harry have celebrated Christmas in Britain with their children, following the family’s move back across the pond from California earlier this year.

According to an exclusive report from People, the couple are hoping to keep the holidays relatively low-key. “She wants the holidays to be as quiet and enjoyable as possible for the family,” a source said.

“Traveling back and forth would be too much for everyone,” the source added. “She doesn’t want to add any more pressure right now.” Ragland, who lives in California, is expected to join the family in the U.K.

Harry also offered a glimpse into their Christmas plans during a surprise appearance on the British morning show This Morning. He joined hosts Alison Hammond and Dermot O’Leary to discuss the upcoming 2027 Invictus Games in Birmingham, the international sporting event for wounded, injured, and sick service members and veterans. He also shared insight into what it’s been like to be back in the U.K. and their plans for the holiday.

Harry shared that Meghan and her mother will likely be taking charge of the holiday cooking. “You know what, it will probably be my wife,” he said. “My wife is very good in the kitchen and so is my mother-in-law, so I’ll leave that to them.” As for the menu, Harry suggested that gumbo could make an appearance alongside a more traditional holiday meal. “Maybe gumbo, yeah, maybe turkey, maybe gumbo,” he said.

“It’s always interesting when you’re living overseas and looking back in, you can see all sorts of stuff happening, and I think now that I’m back everything feels quite familiar and similar,” he said before adding that “obviously there’s lots of change, but as far as London, as far as the U.K., it’s home.”

The family’s holiday plans will look different from the traditional royal Christmas at Sandringham, which Harry and Meghan are not expected to attend. The couple last joined the royal family’s Christmas celebrations there in 2018, the year they married. Archie and Lilibet have never attended.

#6766. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

New research suggests that the value of AI should be measured not only by the work it eliminates, but also by the instructional insight and human connection it preserves.

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.
